# Playtest register

Every report gets the next number and keeps the reporter's words. An item closes only with an
outcome: **done** (commit), **declined** (why), or **moved** (where).

| # | Report | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | "The enemy clock is not totally aligned to the player. When I moved forth and back, the enemy went forth and back too, but stopped with one square of difference." Then: "same happened with moving blocks", and his report (an on-your-move enemy). | **done.** A patrolling piece that hit a wall spent that step turning without moving. It now turns and moves the other way in the same step. His report is `tests/reports/patrol-turn-loses-a-step.json` and replays level. |
| 2 | "When jumping, the enemy only moved once. Should have slid all the way." | **done.** A jump makes each on-your-move piece slide along its patrol, a tile per tick, until something stops it. Still one world step. |
| 3 | "If I have selected Block and I click on a block, I want it to remove that block. It's always an eraser if I'm clicking a certain type with that type selected." | **done.** Any tool: clicking what it places removes it; a drag that starts on one removes along the way. A turret tool on a mounted turret takes only the turret off. |
| 4 | (superseded by 9) "Heavy enemy: if he takes two lasers in a short amount of time, he dies. Like 4 squares of movement." | **done.** Strong enemy: a laser hurts it for 4 of your moves (crack and pips); a second laser in that window kills it. Turret beams count too (my call). |
| 5 | "Add some wall triangles too which redirect all stuff: boxes, enemies, lasers and such. Diagonally." | **done.** Triangle tile, solid in one chosen corner. What enters an open face leaves through the other, turned 90 degrees: you, boxes, heavy boxes, movers, enemies, lasers and beams. A solid side stops things like a wall. |
| 6 | (see 7) "If I slide with my finger I want to add multiple things (replacing what was there) if the first tap was not that type, then remove all instances of that type where I slide." | **done.** A drag starting on the selected type removes that type along the way and nothing else; otherwise it places along the way. Pieces replace pieces and tiles replace tiles; a piece still sits on a floor tile (box on a button) rather than clearing it. |
| 7 | "Nope, tried to slide my finger across many slots and only the first one clicked received a square." | **done.** On a touch screen the drag never looked up the tile under the finger: it read the mouse-hover cell, which is switched off for touch. The phone gate now drags with real touch events; before, it dragged a mouse on a touch-sized page, which could not see this. |
| 8 | "Have the laser turret be a continuous laser, similar to Portal." | **done.** A turret's beam is on all the time, recomputed every tick; it points one barrel at a time and turns clockwise per its clock (real time, on your move) or points your way (same way as you). It kills you (not in the air) and weak enemies; boxes, blocks and strong enemies stop it. New **Receiver** tile: a beam ending on it holds it down like a button of its colour. |
| 9 | "Strong enemy gets immune to all lasers." | **done.** Replaces item 4: no laser hurts it, yours or a turret's; it stops every beam. Only a thrown heavy box kills it. |
| 10 | "I keep editing stuff by accident. Have a neutral, no edit, just move option... when you go from play to edit, that's the one active by default." | **done.** New **Look** tool, first in the palette, selected on start and every time you return to editing. It changes nothing; a tap says what is on that tile; on a phone the page scrolls under it. |
| 11 | "Create one way / two way block. It only allows movement from certain direction(s), and behaves like a wall on the other directions." | **done.** One-way tile with 1 to 4 allowed travel directions: moving onto it and off it only along them, a wall any other way. Applies to you, boxes, heavy boxes, movers, enemies and beams. |
| 12 | "A spring which observes the empty space in its front and when something movable gets there it pushes it. Of course it can't push laser. A death block, anything that collides it is gone. Even strong foes, moving blocks, anything." | **done.** **Spring** tile, solid, facing one way: whatever stops or passes on the tile in front of it is launched its way until something stops it (you, boxes, heavy boxes, movers, enemies). Beams pass it by. **Death block**: anything moving into it is destroyed, you included; a beam stops at it. My calls: you are safe from a spring while airborne; it only launches when the way ahead is free; a launched light box or block stops at you rather than crushing you. |
| 13 | "Jump: let's make it 'hide' instead. It hides until all things associated to that cycle finish their thing. So a sweeping laser wouldn't hit you, nor an enemy. If you unhide and there is an enemy on you, you die. Anything above you: you get squished." | **done.** Space is **Hide**: the world takes its step, on-your-move pieces slide until they stop, and you are off the board meanwhile (nothing meets you, beams pass over, a door can close on you). You come back once nothing it sent sliding is still going, at least 4 ticks (my call, so the hide reads). Any piece on your tile, or a closed door, then squashes you. Replaces the jump: an enemy stepping under you no longer dies. |
| 14 | "A laser sensor, which is like a button but for lasers hitting it only." | **done.** **Laser sensor**: a coloured floor plate, held down while a turret beam crosses it. The beam goes on past it; a piece standing on it keeps the beam off. Your own Laser power is a single flash, so it does not hold one (my call). |
| 15 | "If multiple buttons or sensors of a same colour, all should be triggered continuously for the doors to activate." | **done.** Already the rule for buttons and receivers; sensors join it. A colour's doors are open only while every button, receiver and sensor of that colour is held. |
| 16 | "Doors squish you, weak enemies, weak boxes, but are held by strong enemies, boxes, moving platforms." | **done.** A closing door destroys a weak enemy, a light box or a lone turret in it, and kills you. A strong enemy, a heavy box or a block in it holds the whole colour open. I read "boxes" here as heavy boxes, since light ones are squashed. |
| 17 | "The player may acquire a new status called Armored. Toggle for now. Should be visible when toggled on. Armored: a weak enemy walking into you turns back, you stop in front of one, a block cannot crush you against a wall (it still pushes you), a door cannot close on you (it is held open), and coming back from a hide under a weak enemy, box or block is safe. Strong enemies, heavy boxes, beams and death blocks still kill you. |
| 18 | "The same tap that makes you go invisible also toggles Swimming on and off. Both give you an extra cycle. Create the water block where you can only go through if you are Swimming. You cannot toggle Swimming on water. Colliding with water with swimming off makes you stop there like a rigid block. Swimming should give visual feedback it's under water: refraction, being a bit slower. Laser hitting water damages all things on that same body of water. Weak boxes and enemies collide with water; strong ones and moving platforms don't. No water physics." Answers: one tap does both; in water the tap still hides you; half speed is a real rule; a shocked pool hurts as a direct hit would; a beam passes over water. | **done.** **Swim** power (it was a placeholder) and a **Water** tile. The tap: Cycle hides you, Swim starts or stops swimming (never in water), and either way the world takes one step. Water stops you unless you swim, and stops weak enemies, light boxes and lone turrets; strong enemies, heavy boxes and blocks go through. In water you move a tile every other tick. A turret beam or your laser that touches water shocks every cell joined to it: you die (armored too, not hidden), weak enemies die, strong enemies, blocks and boxes are untouched; the beam goes on. My calls: Swim alone, standing in water, the tap does nothing; heavy boxes count as heavy, so they cross water; on land you wear a blue drop while swimming. |
| 19 | "Hide and swim button shouldn't say so if one of these weren't enabled yet." | **done.** The tap button reads Hide, Swim or Hide / Swim by the powers that are on, and is gone with neither. The keys line follows it. |
| 20 | "Group / tab the tools so we can avoid scrolling on my portrait device." | **done.** Four tabs: Basics, Pieces, Tiles, Switches; a dot marks the tab holding the selected tool, and returning to edit opens Basics on Look. Powers and Status still sit below; they are set once per room. |
| 21 | "Create a sticky puddle. If you go through it you become sticky until you go through water or you stick onto something. If you collide with things that move, you move with them until you release (move in a direction). If you collide with a box it glues into you until you do a movement and it can't keep going with you anymore." Answers: sticking uses it up; you ride moving blocks and sliding boxes (light or heavy); a still heavy box does not stick. | **done.** **Sticky puddle** tile (Tiles tab). Passing it makes you sticky; water washes it off. Sticky, you stop at what you meet and stick to it, which uses the stickiness up: a moving block or a sliding box carries you (a block moving at you still pushes you); any move lets go, even into a wall. A light box glues to you and comes along beside, behind or ahead (ahead it goes first); the first time it cannot follow, it falls off, and ahead you stop against it. A sliding box that reaches you sticky pushes you along and stays glued when it stops. When you cannot follow what you ride, you come off. Hiding lets go. Drawn as goo on you and a goo strand to what you hold. |

| 33 | "Platforms and boxes should also go sticky when moved into a puddle. Enemies too, they can stick to these." The column shared by both boxes is the idea: the first box becomes the second box's wall. Now level 5's known room for that goal in the tests (replaces "no win found"). | 335 | "Your searches are failing quite a lot" | **diagnosed and fixed (2026-10-20).** `tools/searchDiag.mjs <level>` counts what every room the climb scores was. Two causes. **1, a bug, mine:** slice 1 moved the palette maximums from `placementErrors` to `check`, and the search still read only `placementErrors`, so on level 17 it built rooms with two or three inverted doors, scored them 0 and then threw them away when `check` refused them ("not found, closest penalty 0.00"). A room now scores 0 only when `check` itself calls it a win; any other room that would score 0 scores 1 and the climb goes on. Refusing rooms off the counts outright was tried first and cost wins on levels 5 and 12 (over-limit rooms are stepping stones; 3 of 9 runs against 5 of 9), and so did a cost of 5 per piece off the counts (0 of 9). A gate holds every known winning room at 0 and an over-limit room above it. **2, no gradient:** the goals of levels 17 to 19 were all-or-nothing, so a solvable room missing one goal gave the climb nothing to follow. They now get partial credit (`tools/searchScores.mjs`): how near the robot, a beam's end, a beam or a box comes to the tile the goal names, how many relays are lit, how many times the laser was pushed. Measured, 45s runs, seeds 3 to 8: level 17 found a win in 0 of 6 before and 3 of 6 after; level 19 5 of 6 both ways. **Tried and dropped:** longer climbs that stop only when they stall, restarting half the climbs from the best near miss: 4 of 10 runs found a win against 7 of 10 without (seeds 1 and 2, levels 5, 12, 17, 18, 19). Still noisy: with 60s and one seed a search can miss a room that exists, and says so ("not proof"). Re-run on every level at `e339db0`, 60s, seed 1: a win found on 14 of 20 (1-5, 7-10, 12, 14, 18, 19, twocolour); not on 6, 11, 13, 15, 16, 17. At `1d88f3c` the same seed missed 11, 13, 15, 16, 17 too; 6 runs only 2 climbs a minute there (its rooms are slow to solve), so one seed says little about it. Gates: `search` (+4). 3 mutants added. |

Set then released: Q stays 1; Reset then released: Q stays 0; again: stays 1. With no button pressed from the start it flickers `0101` (the circuit's textbook race) until the first press. Colour count: one bit takes 4 (two for its value, two for set and reset), so four colours are exactly one bit, and each more bit needs more colours; wires (row 347) remain what makes a whole machine. |

A test level (block, box, spikes, 7x7 start): with one box no win in 10 minutes of search, and it cannot be: the robot always moves into the tile its box just left, so a box it pushes off spikes leaves the robot on bare spikes, and with one box nothing else can push it. With two boxes: wins in 4 moves, the smallest 3x5 (G, spikes, box, box, robot in one column: the robot pushes the pair up, the front box crosses the spikes onto the goal and the second stays on the spikes); most wins found took 4 moves (72% and 62%), up to 14. So two boxes are needed, one pushing the other, as he expected; the move count is low. **Correction (asked "but is minimum 4?"): no.** 4 was the campaign floor (`MIN_MOVES`, used when a level sets no `steps.min`), so the search could not report less. Without the floor the true minimum is **1 move**: a 1x6 column, top to bottom `. x G B B P` (x spikes); one push up slides both boxes over the goal, the front box over the spikes, the back box stops on the spikes and the robot stops on the goal. | 436 | "Change them to 'all', and add minimum 3 spikes?" then, asked which all: "Every box does both. But yeah let's have minimum 2 spikes" | **changed, measured (2026-10-23).** The two goals are now `everyBoxLeavesSpikes` and `everyBoxLeavesGoal` ("Every box leaves spikes it was on", "Every box leaves the goal it was on"; draft copy): each box in the room must stand there and later stand off it. Search scores give each box credit. Test level: blocks, 2+ boxes, 2+ spikes, no move floor. Three 400s searches: **no win found**. Not proven impossible: a box leaves spikes only when the box behind it in a push takes its place (the robot would land on bare spikes), so the box left on the spikes must later be pushed off by the other box from another side, and the goal must stay unreachable until then. Possible in principle, long and rare, which the random search does not reach. **Hand-sketched win (asked "I sketch one by hand"): 28 moves, 8x9, one shortest path.** Rows: `.#..#..#` `.#......` `#..#....` `....G.#.` `PBBx.#..` `.....#..` `#......#` `....#...` `..#....x` (x spikes). The robot pushes both boxes right: the front box crosses the spikes, the back one stops on them; the front box goes up through the goal, round the left side and the bottom, and back up under the spikes, pushing the other box off them; the robot pushes that box right across the goal and stops on it. The second spike (bottom right) is only there for the minimum of 2. Found by fixing the plan's core by hand and letting a small search place the other blocks; the open search never found one. |

| 558 | (game direction, 2026-11-01) One "single player campaign" button. Inside it you unlock and discover builder levels (today's, side goals unlocked during play, not all at once), puzzle levels (you drive the robot to the end) and challenge levels (new: a semi-built arena, a constrained area, some pieces to use or not, one solution; finish the level so the robot wins on a condition, like chess problems). Beating a builder level unlocks its side goals, the next builder level, and a puzzle or a challenge; a piece used for the first time unlocks it in the editor. A star for each level, side goal, puzzle, challenge and achievement; stars unlock skins (you and blocks), palettes, unusual tools (wiring?) and puzzle or challenge packs. Stars are not a cash currency. Questions: how to prove challenges are fun, viable and working; inspired by Baba Is You's map and Duolingo's track; a diagnosis of similar progressions may help. "Ask me anything." | **direction noted (2026-11-01).** Validation plan answered in chat; questions asked one at a time. |

Random search, 150 arenas each: blocks only, slots 5 found, any tile 0; box + blocks 0 either way; switch + blocks with slots 6. Why: a block only takes paths away, so without a count it can never be what makes the star reachable. Earlier: **right; 570's reasoning was wrong (2026-11-01).** The robot slides until something stops it, so a block is a stopper and can be exactly what makes the star reachable. The earlier search never asked for the star to start out of reach, which is why blocks-only looked hopeless. Re-measured with no move count, 2 blocks, 5x5, star out of reach before any piece: with 8 slots, 3929 of 31291 such arenas have exactly one winning placement; with any floor tile, 0 of 2935 (a stopper usually works in more than one spot). Example (slots): `.P.+. / +G+.+ / ..#.. / ..#+. / .+.+.`, the only win puts blocks under the start and left of the star, 3 moves. So "beat the level, just place the pieces" works with slots; whether it works on any tile needs a search on other arena shapes (more fixed walls, bigger rooms). |

| 577 | (C2 design, screenshot `docs/feedback/2026-11-01-c2-design.jpg`) Feels good like a sudoku but too easy: it solves backwards in three forced steps (a stopper left of the star; the only approach is from the fixed block on the right; so one block bottom right, one top left of it). Many slots are useless and never send the player down a different, nearly-right path. Diagnose and propose before he builds some by hand. | **done (2026-11-01), see 578.** Measured on C1-C5: the robot can come to rest on the star in only ONE way in C1, C2 and C5 (one stopper spot), so the first backward step is forced and every later one follows; C3 and C4 have two. Every slot does matter to the robot somewhere (each one is in some losing placement that sends it sailing over the star), so "useless" is about reasoning, not physics: once the chain is forced the other slots are never weighed. Proposals: (1) the star has 2+ ways to stop, only one completes; (2) the solution's path crosses a slot that looks like a good stopper, so filling it is the trap; (3) trim slots to the ones on some plausible backward chain (trimming never adds a solution); (4) more stops in the solution, 6x6; (5) a piece doing two jobs. Answer pending. |

In Puzzle 4 the button at (2,0) is bait (dead positions 2356 with it, 2110 without). **The button at (0,3) changes nothing reachable:** the whole map of positions is the same with or without it (2462 positions, 2356 dead), so a box on it never traps you more than without it; it reads as idle. | 708 | (screenshot `docs/feedback/2026-11-04-thin-decision-tree.png`, Puzzle 1) "I think that Mashing is not telling me good insights. The thing I'm thinking about is a 'decision tree'. How do we turn that into a metric? is it possible?" | **built (2026-11-04) as 710's undo count**, which replaces Mashing. Earlier note: Answered: yes. Mashing models a player who presses anything, revisits included, which no one does. "Go with the flow" is a player who never goes back to a position they have already been in and picks at random among the moves that lead somewhere new: the chance they reach the berry. Measured with a throwaway script (3000 seeded walks each): Puzzle 1 9%, Puzzles 2, 3 and 4 0%. So it does single out Puzzle 1 as the room you can drift through. Proposed line: "Going with the flow wins 9% of the time." It would replace Mashing (685). A second, exact companion if wanted: how many points on the way have more than one new place to go where only one of them can still reach the berry ("real decisions"). |
| 709 | "It's like a maze. If the dead ends on a maze are short and easy to see and revert, then it may be boring. If they are more complex and further away, you have to be more careful. (or that the berry is still in reach from each), and "The deepest trap lets you walk N moves before you are stuck.", which flashes where that trap starts. Measured: Puzzle 1 has 20 wrong turns and no trap; Puzzle 3 has 32 with 2 traps, the deepest 269 moves; Puzzle 4 has 23 with 2 traps, the deepest 83. Row 716's caution stands: this counts every wrong turn, seen or not. Earlier note: Yes: it turns "how thin is the tree" into "how deep are the wrong turns", which is exact on the metrics map. From each position on the shortest path, every other move is a wrong turn of one of two kinds: a **detour** (the berry is still reachable, just farther: it costs the extra moves to get back) or a **trap** (the berry is gone for good: its depth is how many new positions you can still walk through before you run out, which is how long it takes to notice). Proposed lines: "Wrong turns: 6, and they take 4 moves on average to show." and the deepest one ("The deepest trap lets you walk 9 moves before you are stuck."), flashable to show where it starts. Shallow and obvious reads as boring; deep reads as careful. Go-with-the-flow (708) stays as the one-number summary. |

Proposed line: "Going with the flow, you need about 2 undos." It replaces 708's percentage. |

Measured along the way: pushing for length alone gives corridors of 27-31 moves that a flow player just follows (80-90% of moves on the path, 0-8% undos); pushing for wandering alone gives 8-16 moves. | 716 | "Yeah, not a good level. There is an entire top right chunk of space that is obviously 'do not go there' material. Then, if you go to the left, just follow the path to the goal. Mine was behaving better still." | **done (2026-11-04).** Puzzle 1 is the owner's 10x10 room again (`rooms/puzzle-1.wee` from before 715). The 11x8 room is not kept. **What it teaches about the flow metric (708-710):** the random flow player has no eyes. It wanders into a region any person sees is a dead end and counts every undo there, so a big, obviously closed-off chunk inflates the undo share; that is exactly what the search rewarded. A person skips what they can see is closed and then follows the corridor. So the undo share measures "how much room there is to get lost", not "how lost a person gets", and searching on it alone breeds rooms with one obvious decoy and one obvious path. Before 708 is built or used to search: count only wrong turns a person could not rule out from where they stand (a branch whose dead end is not visible in a straight line), or weight undos by how far the dead end was from the choice (709's depth). Owner, after: moving its start one tile right adds a move, with the first move forced: so the search had not even reached its own best, and a forced move adds length without a decision. Move count alone rewards filler; a search target should count decisions, not moves. |
| 717 | "A good level has piece reusage. It's not bumping through a straight path to the end." | **built (2026-11-04), metric.** "Reuse: N moves lean on K blocks, the busiest M times.", counted on one shortest answer (a piece counts as itself, wherever it stands). Measured: Puzzle 1, 19 moves on 11 blocks, busiest 4; Puzzle 2, 22 on 4, busiest 15; Puzzle 3, 28 on 10, busiest 10; Puzzle 4, 29 on 10, busiest 12. Not yet a search target. Earlier note: A room is better when the answer leans on the same blocks again and again (a block stopped against from several sides, at several points), not one fresh block per move. Measurable exactly on the answer: for each move, the tile that stops the slide; then how many distinct stoppers carry the whole answer and how many times the busiest is used. Proposed line: "Reuse: 19 moves lean on 7 blocks, the busiest 4 times." (one block per move reads as a straight path; few blocks, many uses reads as reuse). It is the room-wide form of the side goal "touch the same block 4 times" (193), and with 716 it is a better target for any room search than raw moves: reward reuse and decisions, not length. |

A good room scores high on all three, with 709's traps deep enough to matter and few forced moves (716). Next: show the three as notes (reuse is already there), measure them on this room and P1 to P4, then use them as the score for the next generated P1, under 716's rule that no part of the room is an obvious no-go.
